    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yes, thank you. Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve realized I’m basically arranging my entire life—or my house, really—around not seeing spiders. \n\nWhich is just so silly, because I teach secondary school, you know? I deal with chaotic teenagers all day long. Just last week, one of my Year 10 boys tried to smuggle a stray kitten into the classroom in his backpack. A kitten! Can you imagine the disruption? We were supposed to be covering the Industrial Revolution, but of course, that went completely out the window the second it started meowing. They’re good kids, though, just so easily distracted at that age. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, my house. It's just getting out of hand. I put off doing the laundry for days and days because the machine is down in the basement. I avoid the garden shed entirely. I’ve even started paying this local handyman to come over and do these tiny little jobs around the house—things I know perfectly well how to do myself!—just because I physically cannot make myself go into those spaces.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of spiders themselves or something they might do, like bite you?\n\nPatient: Well, I know a spider just sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I mean, my aunt used to have this terribly old cottage down in Cornwall—beautiful place, right on the coast, but oh, the draughts! We used to go there every August when I was a girl, and you’d have to wear two jumpers just to sit in the lounge. She had this massive collection of antique porcelain teacups that she kept packed away in these dusty cardboard boxes in the spare room. My mother was always trying to get her to display them properly, but she never did. \n\nBut anyway, the boxes... I think that's why I can't handle things like that now. It's not that I think they'll bite me, really. But the moment I picture one dropping out of a box like that, or just sitting there near my washer, it feels... enormous. And just so threatening in a way I’m honestly quite embarrassed to admit.\n\nDoctor: Is it the appearance of the spiders themselves that you find threatening, or the idea of them being in a particular place or situation?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s… I suppose it’s the appearance of them, really. The way they move, those long legs, it just makes my skin crawl. It’s funny you mention situations, though, because it reminds me of this one time at school. We were having a staff meeting—this was back when Mr. Henderson was still the headmaster, lovely man, but he insisted on having these meetings in the old library. The heating in there never worked properly, and the chairs were those awful wooden ones that give you a backache if you sit in them for more than ten minutes. Anyway, I was sitting near the window, and I saw something out of the corner of my eye. \n\nBut, oh, wait, what were you asking? Right, the appearance. Yes, it’s definitely how they look. I don’t think it matters where they are, really. If I see one, even if it’s outside or on the telly, I just… I can’t look at it. It’s just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself reacting similarly to other creatures, like insects or small animals, or is it specifically spiders that cause this reaction?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's just spiders. I'm perfectly fine with other insects. Well, mostly. I mean, nobody likes a wasp, do they? We had a terrible wasp nest at the school last summer, right near the canteen entrance. The caretaker, Gary—bless him, he’s been there forever and he’s practically deaf in one ear—he went at it with this spray he bought at the hardware store. Just completely soaked it. The kids were absolutely terrified, screaming and running about with their lunch trays. It was chaos. I had to usher my Year 8s back into the main hall and we ended up eating our sandwiches sitting on the floor of the gymnasium. \n\nBut no, I wasn't afraid of them the way I am with spiders. I just didn't want anyone getting stung. With spiders, it's... it's a completely different feeling. It's just them. Just the spiders.\n\nDoctor: Have you started avoiding social events or activities outside of your home because of your fear of encountering spiders?\n\nPatient: Well, social events... no, not really. I mean, I still go out to dinner with my friends, and I went to my niece's wedding last month. Beautiful ceremony, by the way. It was out in the Cotswolds, and they had this massive marquee set up on the lawn. The catering was lovely, though I must say the beef was a bit overdone for my taste. I always tell my sister she ought to use a meat thermometer, but she never listens. She’s terribly stubborn like that. \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying? Right, avoiding things. It’s not so much outside the home that’s the problem. It’s *my* home. Owning this house has just become so much more expensive and difficult to manage lately. I’m having to pay the neighbor's boy to come over and do the gardening because I just can't bring myself to go near the shed or the hedges. And the chores inside... well, things tend to pile up. I won't go down to the basement at all anymore, which is where the main storage is, so I've got boxes sitting in the hallway. It's just... I'm avoiding parts of my own property that I really need to use, just in case there's one of them lurking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided traveling or going on vacation because you might encounter spiders in a new or unfamiliar environment?\n\nPatient: Traveling? No, I wouldn't say I've avoided that. I do love a good holiday, especially getting away during the half-term break when the weather here is just so miserable. Actually, my friend Susan and I went to Mallorca just this past Easter. We stayed in this lovely little boutique hotel away from the main tourist strip. It was supposed to be quiet and relaxing, but wouldn't you know it, they were doing construction on the building right next door. The drilling started at eight in the morning every single day! Susan was absolutely furious. She actually went down to reception in her dressing gown to complain, but the poor lad at the desk barely spoke a word of English and just kept offering her extra towels. We ended up spending most of our days down by the marina just to escape the noise. They had this wonderful little cafe there that did the most incredible almond cake... \n\nAnyway, sorry, I'm wandering off again. What was the question? Oh, avoiding vacations. No, I don't really think about them when I'm booking a trip. I just assume hotels will be clean and sorted, I suppose. It's really just my own house where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down an opportunity or left a situation because you thought there might be spiders present?\n\nPatient: Turned down an opportunity... well, it’s really just what I was saying about my own house. Honestly, that’s what brings me in to see you. I just keep arranging my entire life at home around not seeing them. \n\nLike the laundry, for instance. I put off doing it for days on end because the machine is down in the basement. I actually ended up buying new knickers at Marks & Spencer last Tuesday because I just couldn't face going down the stairs to wash mine! Though I have to say, M&S has really gone downhill lately, hasn't it? I remember when their cotton was so much softer. Now it's all these synthetic blends that go bobbly after just one wash. My mother used to swear by their knitwear, she had a cardigan in practically every color. She wore this lovely lemon yellow one to my graduation... \n\nOh, but anyway, where was I? Right, the house. Yes, I avoid the basement, I avoid the garden shed completely, like I mentioned. I even pay people to do small jobs that I know perfectly well how to do myself, simply because I can’t make myself go into those spaces. Like last week, the latch on the back gate was sticking. I know how to fix it, I’ve got the WD-40 sitting right on the kitchen counter, but the gate is next to all that thick ivy. So I paid a local handyman forty quid to do a five-minute job. Forty pounds! Just because I couldn't go near the fence. It's just getting ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided a specific room or area in your home for an extended period because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Well, like I just said, the basement is completely off-limits. I haven't set foot down there in absolute ages. It actually reminds me a bit of the old boiler room at the school where I teach. I teach Year 9 geography, you know, and the kids are always trying to sneak down into the boiler room during break time. Just last term, we had this one boy, Liam—bright lad, but always finding trouble—who got caught down there smoking with some older boys. The headteacher was absolutely livid. He called a full staff meeting on a Friday afternoon, which is just the worst possible time, isn't it? Everyone just wants to get home and start their weekend. I had plans to meet my sister for a carvery at the local pub, and I ended up being nearly an hour late. They'd run out of the roast pork by the time I got there, which is a terrible shame because their crackling is usually absolutely fantastic... \n\nAnyway, I'm sorry, I've lost the thread again. Did you ask about other rooms? No, it's really just the basement and the shed, like I mentioned. I haven't been avoiding anywhere else inside the house, if that's what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you have completely stopped doing because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Well, it's like I was saying before with the laundry and the gate. I've basically stopped doing any sort of maintenance or chores that take me near the basement or the garden shed. It's just made owning this house so much harder to manage. And far more expensive! I'm constantly having to hire people for the silliest little jobs.\n\nActually, speaking of expensive, you wouldn't believe the quote I got for getting the gutters cleaned last month. Absolute daylight robbery! The chap came round in this terribly flashy van—one of those new electric ones, you know, completely silent. You don't even hear them coming down the street. It’s quite dangerous, really. My neighbor Mrs. Higgins nearly stepped right out in front of a delivery van the other day because she didn't hear it. She’s getting on a bit, poor dear, and her hearing isn't what it used to be. I’ve told her time and time again she needs to go and get her ears syringed, but she insists on trying these home remedies she reads about in magazines. The other week she was putting warm olive oil in them, which just sounds horribly messy to me...\n\nOh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Sorry. But yes, the daily activities... I just let chores back up terribly, like the washing, and I completely avoid parts of my own property that I really ought to be using. It's just become a massive headache to keep the place running.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the spiders, such as their legs, eyes, or movement, that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I've never stopped to analyze exactly what part of them it is. Just the whole... spider-ness of them, I suppose? I just know I can't look at them. I mean, if I'm close enough to see their eyes, things have gone horribly wrong already! \n\nIt's a bit like when my sister insists on showing me photos of her new dog. She got this pug a few months ago—Archie, she calls him—and she's always zooming in on his face on her phone. \"Look at those big eyes,\" she says. But honestly, those dogs just look like they're struggling to breathe, don't they? I read an article in the Sunday paper about how they've been bred to have those flat faces, and it causes all sorts of health problems. The vet bills must be astronomical. I told her she should have got a sensible dog, like a Labrador or maybe a nice little terrier. We had a terrier when we were growing up, a Jack Russell named Barnaby. He was an absolute terror for digging up the flowerbeds, though. My dad was always furious because he spent his weekends trying to cultivate these prize-winning dahlias...\n\nSorry, what did you ask me? Right, the spiders. No, there isn't one specific thing about them. It's just the whole creature that makes my skin crawl.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any hobbies or leisure activities because of your fear of encountering spiders?\n\nPatient: Well, not really, no. I mean, most of my hobbies are indoors anyway, like reading and baking. I do love a good bake. Actually, I was just making a Victoria sponge for the school summer fete last weekend. It’s usually such a lovely event, but the weather was absolutely dreadful this year. It just poured down right as they were setting up the tombola. The poor PTA chair, Susan, was running around like a headless chicken trying to get the gazebos up. She’s a lovely woman, Susan, but terribly disorganized. She once forgot to book the bouncy castle until the day before! Can you imagine the uproar? The children would have been absolutely devastated. Luckily her husband knows a chap who runs a party hire business, so they managed to sort it out at the eleventh hour. He’s a plumber, her husband, or maybe an electrician... I remember he came round in a van once. Not one of those silent electric ones, thankfully. \n\nOh, dear, sorry. I've drifted off again, haven't I? What were we talking about? Hobbies, yes. No, I haven't stopped doing anything I do for fun because of the spiders. It's really just the basement and the shed that are the problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar reaction to any other animals that have multiple legs, like insects or centipedes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't love creepy-crawlies in general, but a centipede or a beetle doesn't send me into a panic like a spider does. I might just swat it with a magazine or something. Usually one of those home and garden magazines, actually. \n\nI've got stacks of them by the sofa. I keep meaning to go through them and cut out the recipes, but I never get around to it. There was this one issue that had a fantastic spread on different types of marmalade. Seville orange, ginger and lemon, even a grapefruit one! I tried making the grapefruit one, but it turned out terribly bitter. My husband—well, ex-husband now—he used to love bitter marmalade. He'd slather it on his toast every morning. Honestly, the amount of butter he used, it's a wonder his cholesterol wasn't through the roof. He was always going on these fad diets, though. One month it was all cabbage soup, the next he was only eating things that were green. I remember he tried to make a green smoothie once and the blender broke...\n\nSorry, what was the question? Other insects? Right. No, it's really just the spiders that cause this whole... reaction. Everything else is just a bit of a nuisance, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear or reaction to any other type of creature that isn't a spider?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, snakes are a bit unnerving, aren't they? But I wouldn't say I have an intense fear of them. I saw one on a school trip once, actually. We took Year 8 to this nature reserve in the Peak District. Beautiful place, really, all rolling hills and dry stone walls. But the bus journey was a nightmare. Little Tommy Jenkins threw up his packed lunch before we'd even reached the motorway. It was egg sandwiches, too. The smell was just awful. The driver, Colin, wasn't best pleased, I can tell you. He was a grumpy sort of chap anyway, always complaining about his sciatica. He had this special cushion he sat on, looked like a giant doughnut... \n\nAnyway, where was I? Oh, the snake. Yes, the guide pointed out an adder sunning itself on a rock. Some of the girls screamed, of course, but I just thought it looked a bit lazy. Not like a spider at all. So no, nothing else gives me that awful panicked feeling. Just the spiders.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, my friends know I don't like them, but it hasn't caused any real issues. My friend Margaret, she's actually terrified of birds. Can you believe it? Pigeons, seagulls, even little robins. We were having tea on her patio last summer—she has this gorgeous wrought-iron patio set she got at an estate sale. It took her husband three weekends to sand down and repaint it. He used this lovely duck-egg blue, though I told her a classic white might have been better for the garden. Anyway, we were having tea, and a blackbird landed on the fence, and she practically threw her Earl Grey over her shoulder trying to get inside. Ruined a perfectly good linen blouse. It was one of those M&S ones, you know, the ones that wash so well but need a good iron. I always say, if you're going to buy linen, you have to commit to the ironing board. My mother used to starch everything. Pillowcases, tablecloths, even my father's handkerchiefs... \n\nOh, I've done it again, haven't I? I'm so sorry. What was the question? Relationships? Yes, no, my fear hasn't ruined any friendships or anything like that. It's just a personal nuisance, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt afraid of other arachnids, like ticks or scorpions, in a similar way to how you feel about spiders?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say I have. Ticks are nasty little things, aren't they? Carrying diseases and such. My sister's dog, a lovely golden retriever named Barnaby, got a tick once after they went walking in the New Forest. Oh, the New Forest is stunning in the autumn. The leaves turn these brilliant shades of copper and gold. We used to rent a cottage down there when the kids were small. It had this thatched roof that looked like something out of a fairy tale, but it was terribly drafty in the winter. My daughter, Sarah, she always had to wear two pairs of socks just to walk across the kitchen floor. She was a bit sensitive to the cold, Sarah. Still is, actually. She moved to Scotland last year for university, Edinburgh, and she's constantly complaining about the wind off the North Sea. I told her to buy a proper down coat, but she insisted on this trendy little cropped jacket thing that barely covers her waist. Kids today, they'll freeze for fashion, won't they? I remember when I was her age, I had this awful bright pink parka... \n\nOh, sorry. Ticks and scorpions, you said? No, I've never seen a scorpion, thank goodness, and ticks are just gross, but they don't make my heart pound or anything. It's really just the spiders.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any activities outside of your home due to the possibility of encountering spiders?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I avoid things outside the house. I mean, they're mostly in dark corners indoors, aren't they? Or sheds. I do avoid the shed. But out and about, no. I still go to the garden centre every Sunday. It's a bit of a ritual, actually. They do a lovely carvery on a Sunday afternoon. The beef is always perfectly pink, and the Yorkshire puddings are massive—proper homemade ones, not those frozen things you get in the supermarket. I tried making my own once, but the batter just didn't rise. My mother-in-law, she had the magic touch with Yorkshires. She'd get the fat smoking hot before pouring the batter in. She used beef dripping, which I suppose isn't very healthy these days, what with cholesterol and everything. My GP was just telling me last month I need to watch my saturated fats. He suggested I switch to olive oil for everything, but you can't roast potatoes in olive oil, can you? They just don't get that lovely crispy edge. I usually buy Maris Pipers for roasting, though King Edwards are good too if you can find them...\n\nOh dear, I'm rambling about potatoes now. I do apologize. But yes, to answer your question, I don't avoid going out or doing activities because of the spiders. Just the shed, really.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the way spiders look or move that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if it's one specific thing. I mean, maybe it's all those legs? Just the way they scuttle across the floor, it's... ugh. It makes my skin crawl. It reminds me of this wind-up toy my brother had when we were kids. It was this horrid little tin crab, painted bright red. He used to wind it up and set it loose in the hallway just as I was coming out of the bathroom. He was a terror, my brother. Always playing pranks. Once, he put a plastic snake in my school bag right before a maths test. Mr. Henderson, our teacher, was not amused. He was a very strict man, Mr. Henderson. Always wore a tweed jacket with those leather elbow patches, even in the middle of July. He smelled faintly of chalk dust and peppermint. I actually saw him at the greengrocer's a few years ago. He must be in his eighties now, but he still had that same stern look about him. He was buying a massive cabbage. I remember wondering what one man could possibly do with a cabbage that size. You can only make so much coleslaw, can't you? And cabbage soup is just dreary. I tried the cabbage soup diet back in the nineties—everyone was doing it—and I swear I didn't lose a pound, just felt miserable and smelled like a boiled dinner for a week...\n\nOh, I've drifted off again, haven't I? I'm so sorry. Legs. Yes, I think it's the legs, and the way they move so fast. But I couldn't say for sure if it's just that. It's just the whole... spider-ness of them, I suppose.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Just because I couldn't go near the fence. It's just getting ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided a specific room or area in your home for an extended period because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Well, like I just said, the basement is completely off-limits. I haven't set foot down there in absolute ages. It actually reminds me a bit of the old boiler room at the school where I teach. I teach Year 9 geography, you know, and the kids are always trying to sneak down into the boiler room during break time. Just last term, we had this one boy, Liam—bright lad, but always finding trouble—who got caught down there smoking with some older boys. The headteacher was absolutely livid. He called a full staff meeting on a Friday afternoon, which is just the worst possible time, isn't it? Everyone just wants to get home and start their weekend. I had plans to meet my sister for a carvery at the local pub, and I ended up being nearly an hour late. They'd run out of the roast pork by the time I got there, which is a terrible shame because their crackling is usually absolutely fantastic... \n\nAnyway, I'm sorry, I've lost the thread again. Did you ask about other rooms? No, it's really just the basement and the shed, like I mentioned. I haven't been avoiding anywhere else inside the house, if that's what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you have completely stopped doing because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Well, it's like I was saying before with the laundry and the gate. I've basically stopped doing any sort of maintenance or chores that take me near the basement or the garden shed. It's just made owning this house so much harder to manage. And far more expensive! I'm constantly having to hire people for the silliest little jobs.\n\nActually, speaking of expensive, you wouldn't believe the quote I got for getting the gutters cleaned last month. Absolute daylight robbery! The chap came round in this terribly flashy van—one of those new electric ones, you know, completely silent. You don't even hear them coming down the street. It’s quite dangerous, really. My neighbor Mrs. Higgins nearly stepped right out in front of a delivery van the other day because she didn't hear it. She’s getting on a bit, poor dear, and her hearing isn't what it used to be. I’ve told her time and time again she needs to go and get her ears syringed, but she insists on trying these home remedies she reads about in magazines. The other week she was putting warm olive oil in them, which just sounds horribly messy to me...\n\nOh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Sorry. But yes, the daily activities... I just let chores back up terribly, like the washing, and I completely avoid parts of my own property that I really ought to be using. It's just become a massive headache to keep the place running.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the spiders, such as their legs, eyes, or movement, that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I've never stopped to analyze exactly what part of them it is. Just the whole... spider-ness of them, I suppose? I just know I can't look at them. I mean, if I'm close enough to see their eyes, things have gone horribly wrong already! \n\nIt's a bit like when my sister insists on showing me photos of her new dog. She got this pug a few months ago—Archie, she calls him—and she's always zooming in on his face on her phone. \"Look at those big eyes,\" she says. But honestly, those dogs just look like they're struggling to breathe, don't they? I read an article in the Sunday paper about how they've been bred to have those flat faces, and it causes all sorts of health problems. The vet bills must be astronomical. I told her she should have got a sensible dog, like a Labrador or maybe a nice little terrier. We had a terrier when we were growing up, a Jack Russell named Barnaby. He was an absolute terror for digging up the flowerbeds, though. My dad was always furious because he spent his weekends trying to cultivate these prize-winning dahlias...\n\nSorry, what did you ask me? Right, the spiders. No, there isn't one specific thing about them. It's just the whole creature that makes my skin crawl.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any hobbies or leisure activities because of your fear of encountering spiders?\n\nPatient: Well, not really, no. I mean, most of my hobbies are indoors anyway, like reading and baking. I do love a good bake. Actually, I was just making a Victoria sponge for the school summer fete last weekend. It’s usually such a lovely event, but the weather was absolutely dreadful this year. It just poured down right as they were setting up the tombola. The poor PTA chair, Susan, was running around like a headless chicken trying to get the gazebos up. She’s a lovely woman, Susan, but terribly disorganized. She once forgot to book the bouncy castle until the day before! Can you imagine the uproar? The children would have been absolutely devastated. Luckily her husband knows a chap who runs a party hire business, so they managed to sort it out at the eleventh hour. He’s a plumber, her husband, or maybe an electrician... I remember he came round in a van once. Not one of those silent electric ones, thankfully. \n\nOh, dear, sorry. I've drifted off again, haven't I? What were we talking about? Hobbies, yes. No, I haven't stopped doing anything I do for fun because of the spiders. It's really just the basement and the shed that are the problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar reaction to any other animals that have multiple legs, like insects or centipedes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't love creepy-crawlies in general, but a centipede or a beetle doesn't send me into a panic like a spider does. I might just swat it with a magazine or something. Usually one of those home and garden magazines, actually. \n\nI've got stacks of them by the sofa. I keep meaning to go through them and cut out the recipes, but I never get around to it. There was this one issue that had a fantastic spread on different types of marmalade. Seville orange, ginger and lemon, even a grapefruit one! I tried making the grapefruit one, but it turned out terribly bitter. My husband—well, ex-husband now—he used to love bitter marmalade. He'd slather it on his toast every morning. Honestly, the amount of butter he used, it's a wonder his cholesterol wasn't through the roof. He was always going on these fad diets, though. One month it was all cabbage soup, the next he was only eating things that were green. I remember he tried to make a green smoothie once and the blender broke...\n\nSorry, what was the question? Other insects? Right. No, it's really just the spiders that cause this whole... reaction. Everything else is just a bit of a nuisance, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear or reaction to any other type of creature that isn't a spider?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, snakes are a bit unnerving, aren't they? But I wouldn't say I have an intense fear of them. I saw one on a school trip once, actually. We took Year 8 to this nature reserve in the Peak District. Beautiful place, really, all rolling hills and dry stone walls. But the bus journey was a nightmare. Little Tommy Jenkins threw up his packed lunch before we'd even reached the motorway. It was egg sandwiches, too. The smell was just awful. The driver, Colin, wasn't best pleased, I can tell you. He was a grumpy sort of chap anyway, always complaining about his sciatica. He had this special cushion he sat on, looked like a giant doughnut... \n\nAnyway, where was I? Oh, the snake. Yes, the guide pointed out an adder sunning itself on a rock. Some of the girls screamed, of course, but I just thought it looked a bit lazy. Not like a spider at all. So no, nothing else gives me that awful panicked feeling. Just the spiders.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, my friends know I don't like them, but it hasn't caused any real issues. My friend Margaret, she's actually terrified of birds. Can you believe it? Pigeons, seagulls, even little robins. We were having tea on her patio last summer—she has this gorgeous wrought-iron patio set she got at an estate sale. It took her husband three weekends to sand down and repaint it. He used this lovely duck-egg blue, though I told her a classic white might have been better for the garden. Anyway, we were having tea, and a blackbird landed on the fence, and she practically threw her Earl Grey over her shoulder trying to get inside. Ruined a perfectly good linen blouse. It was one of those M&S ones, you know, the ones that wash so well but need a good iron. I always say, if you're going to buy linen, you have to commit to the ironing board. My mother used to starch everything. Pillowcases, tablecloths, even my father's handkerchiefs... \n\nOh, I've done it again, haven't I? I'm so sorry. What was the question? Relationships? Yes, no, my fear hasn't ruined any friendships or anything like that. It's just a personal nuisance, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt afraid of other arachnids, like ticks or scorpions, in a similar way to how you feel about spiders?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say I have. Ticks are nasty little things, aren't they? Carrying diseases and such. My sister's dog, a lovely golden retriever named Barnaby, got a tick once after they went walking in the New Forest. Oh, the New Forest is stunning in the autumn. The leaves turn these brilliant shades of copper and gold. We used to rent a cottage down there when the kids were small. It had this thatched roof that looked like something out of a fairy tale, but it was terribly drafty in the winter. My daughter, Sarah, she always had to wear two pairs of socks just to walk across the kitchen floor. She was a bit sensitive to the cold, Sarah. Still is, actually. She moved to Scotland last year for university, Edinburgh, and she's constantly complaining about the wind off the North Sea. I told her to buy a proper down coat, but she insisted on this trendy little cropped jacket thing that barely covers her waist. Kids today, they'll freeze for fashion, won't they? I remember when I was her age, I had this awful bright pink parka... \n\nOh, sorry. Ticks and scorpions, you said? No, I've never seen a scorpion, thank goodness, and ticks are just gross, but they don't make my heart pound or anything. It's really just the spiders.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any activities outside of your home due to the possibility of encountering spiders?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I avoid things outside the house. I mean, they're mostly in dark corners indoors, aren't they? Or sheds. I do avoid the shed. But out and about, no. I still go to the garden centre every Sunday. It's a bit of a ritual, actually. They do a lovely carvery on a Sunday afternoon. The beef is always perfectly pink, and the Yorkshire puddings are massive—proper homemade ones, not those frozen things you get in the supermarket. I tried making my own once, but the batter just didn't rise. My mother-in-law, she had the magic touch with Yorkshires. She'd get the fat smoking hot before pouring the batter in. She used beef dripping, which I suppose isn't very healthy these days, what with cholesterol and everything. My GP was just telling me last month I need to watch my saturated fats. He suggested I switch to olive oil for everything, but you can't roast potatoes in olive oil, can you? They just don't get that lovely crispy edge. I usually buy Maris Pipers for roasting, though King Edwards are good too if you can find them...\n\nOh dear, I'm rambling about potatoes now. I do apologize. But yes, to answer your question, I don't avoid going out or doing activities because of the spiders. Just the shed, really.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the way spiders look or move that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if it's one specific thing. I mean, maybe it's all those legs? Just the way they scuttle across the floor, it's... ugh. It makes my skin crawl. It reminds me of this wind-up toy my brother had when we were kids. It was this horrid little tin crab, painted bright red. He used to wind it up and set it loose in the hallway just as I was coming out of the bathroom. He was a terror, my brother. Always playing pranks. Once, he put a plastic snake in my school bag right before a maths test. Mr. Henderson, our teacher, was not amused. He was a very strict man, Mr. Henderson. Always wore a tweed jacket with those leather elbow patches, even in the middle of July. He smelled faintly of chalk dust and peppermint. I actually saw him at the greengrocer's a few years ago. He must be in his eighties now, but he still had that same stern look about him. He was buying a massive cabbage. I remember wondering what one man could possibly do with a cabbage that size. You can only make so much coleslaw, can't you? And cabbage soup is just dreary. I tried the cabbage soup diet back in the nineties—everyone was doing it—and I swear I didn't lose a pound, just felt miserable and smelled like a boiled dinner for a week...\n\nOh, I've drifted off again, haven't I? I'm so sorry. Legs. Yes, I think it's the legs, and the way they move so fast. But I couldn't say for sure if it's just that. It's just the whole... spider-ness of them, I suppose.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Daddy long legs? Oh, no, not at all. They're sort of... delicate, aren't they? Almost like they're just hovering there. They don't have that heavy, scuttling quality. We used to get them all the time in the corners of the classroom when I first started teaching. I was at this lovely little primary school in the Cotswolds back then. The building was Victorian, high ceilings, enormous windows that let in all this glorious light, but my goodness, the draughts! We had a caretaker, Mr. Higgins, who was always trying to patch up the window frames with this awful grey putty. He was a character, Mr. Higgins. He used to bring his Jack Russell, Buster, to work with him. Buster would just sleep under the radiator in the staff room all day. He was the fattest little dog I've ever seen. I think the dinner ladies were sneaking him sausages. They did a wonderful toad-in-the-hole on Thursdays, the dinner ladies. The kids absolutely loved it, though I always found the gravy a bit too salty. I have to be careful with my salt intake these days, you know. Blood pressure and all that. My husband bought me one of those fancy monitors you can use at home, but it always squeezes my arm far too tight...

Oh, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I do apologize. But no, daddy long legs don't bother me in the slightest. Just the spiders.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yes, thank you. Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve realized I’m basically arranging my entire life—or my house, really—around not seeing spiders. \n\nWhich is just so silly, because I teach secondary school, you know? I deal with chaotic teenagers all day long. Just last week, one of my Year 10 boys tried to smuggle a stray kitten into the classroom in his backpack. A kitten! Can you imagine the disruption? We were supposed to be covering the Industrial Revolution, but of course, that went completely out the window the second it started meowing. They’re good kids, though, just so easily distracted at that age. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, my house. It's just getting out of hand. I put off doing the laundry for days and days because the machine is down in the basement. I avoid the garden shed entirely. I’ve even started paying this local handyman to come over and do these tiny little jobs around the house—things I know perfectly well how to do myself!—just because I physically cannot make myself go into those spaces.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of spiders themselves or something they might do, like bite you?\n\nPatient: Well, I know a spider just sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I mean, my aunt used to have this terribly old cottage down in Cornwall—beautiful place, right on the coast, but oh, the draughts! We used to go there every August when I was a girl, and you’d have to wear two jumpers just to sit in the lounge. She had this massive collection of antique porcelain teacups that she kept packed away in these dusty cardboard boxes in the spare room. My mother was always trying to get her to display them properly, but she never did. \n\nBut anyway, the boxes... I think that's why I can't handle things like that now. It's not that I think they'll bite me, really. But the moment I picture one dropping out of a box like that, or just sitting there near my washer, it feels... enormous. And just so threatening in a way I’m honestly quite embarrassed to admit.\n\nDoctor: Is it the appearance of the spiders themselves that you find threatening, or the idea of them being in a particular place or situation?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s… I suppose it’s the appearance of them, really. The way they move, those long legs, it just makes my skin crawl. It’s funny you mention situations, though, because it reminds me of this one time at school. We were having a staff meeting—this was back when Mr. Henderson was still the headmaster, lovely man, but he insisted on having these meetings in the old library. The heating in there never worked properly, and the chairs were those awful wooden ones that give you a backache if you sit in them for more than ten minutes. Anyway, I was sitting near the window, and I saw something out of the corner of my eye. \n\nBut, oh, wait, what were you asking? Right, the appearance. Yes, it’s definitely how they look. I don’t think it matters where they are, really. If I see one, even if it’s outside or on the telly, I just… I can’t look at it. It’s just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself reacting similarly to other creatures, like insects or small animals, or is it specifically spiders that cause this reaction?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's just spiders. I'm perfectly fine with other insects. Well, mostly. I mean, nobody likes a wasp, do they? We had a terrible wasp nest at the school last summer, right near the canteen entrance. The caretaker, Gary—bless him, he’s been there forever and he’s practically deaf in one ear—he went at it with this spray he bought at the hardware store. Just completely soaked it. The kids were absolutely terrified, screaming and running about with their lunch trays. It was chaos. I had to usher my Year 8s back into the main hall and we ended up eating our sandwiches sitting on the floor of the gymnasium. \n\nBut no, I wasn't afraid of them the way I am with spiders. I just didn't want anyone getting stung. With spiders, it's... it's a completely different feeling. It's just them. Just the spiders.\n\nDoctor: Have you started avoiding social events or activities outside of your home because of your fear of encountering spiders?\n\nPatient: Well, social events... no, not really. I mean, I still go out to dinner with my friends, and I went to my niece's wedding last month. Beautiful ceremony, by the way. It was out in the Cotswolds, and they had this massive marquee set up on the lawn. The catering was lovely, though I must say the beef was a bit overdone for my taste. I always tell my sister she ought to use a meat thermometer, but she never listens. She’s terribly stubborn like that. \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying? Right, avoiding things. It’s not so much outside the home that’s the problem. It’s *my* home. Owning this house has just become so much more expensive and difficult to manage lately. I’m having to pay the neighbor's boy to come over and do the gardening because I just can't bring myself to go near the shed or the hedges. And the chores inside... well, things tend to pile up. I won't go down to the basement at all anymore, which is where the main storage is, so I've got boxes sitting in the hallway. It's just... I'm avoiding parts of my own property that I really need to use, just in case there's one of them lurking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided traveling or going on vacation because you might encounter spiders in a new or unfamiliar environment?\n\nPatient: Traveling? No, I wouldn't say I've avoided that. I do love a good holiday, especially getting away during the half-term break when the weather here is just so miserable. Actually, my friend Susan and I went to Mallorca just this past Easter. We stayed in this lovely little boutique hotel away from the main tourist strip. It was supposed to be quiet and relaxing, but wouldn't you know it, they were doing construction on the building right next door. The drilling started at eight in the morning every single day! Susan was absolutely furious. She actually went down to reception in her dressing gown to complain, but the poor lad at the desk barely spoke a word of English and just kept offering her extra towels. We ended up spending most of our days down by the marina just to escape the noise. They had this wonderful little cafe there that did the most incredible almond cake... \n\nAnyway, sorry, I'm wandering off again. What was the question? Oh, avoiding vacations. No, I don't really think about them when I'm booking a trip. I just assume hotels will be clean and sorted, I suppose. It's really just my own house where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down an opportunity or left a situation because you thought there might be spiders present?\n\nPatient: Turned down an opportunity... well, it’s really just what I was saying about my own house. Honestly, that’s what brings me in to see you. I just keep arranging my entire life at home around not seeing them. \n\nLike the laundry, for instance. I put off doing it for days on end because the machine is down in the basement. I actually ended up buying new knickers at Marks & Spencer last Tuesday because I just couldn't face going down the stairs to wash mine! Though I have to say, M&S has really gone downhill lately, hasn't it? I remember when their cotton was so much softer. Now it's all these synthetic blends that go bobbly after just one wash. My mother used to swear by their knitwear, she had a cardigan in practically every color. She wore this lovely lemon yellow one to my graduation... \n\nOh, but anyway, where was I? Right, the house. Yes, I avoid the basement, I avoid the garden shed completely, like I mentioned. I even pay people to do small jobs that I know perfectly well how to do myself, simply because I can’t make myself go into those spaces. Like last week, the latch on the back gate was sticking. I know how to fix it, I’ve got the WD-40 sitting right on the kitchen counter, but the gate is next to all that thick ivy. So I paid a local handyman forty quid to do a five-minute job. Forty pounds! Just because I couldn't go near the fence. It's just getting ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided a specific room or area in your home for an extended period because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Well, like I just said, the basement is completely off-limits. I haven't set foot down there in absolute ages. It actually reminds me a bit of the old boiler room at the school where I teach. I teach Year 9 geography, you know, and the kids are always trying to sneak down into the boiler room during break time. Just last term, we had this one boy, Liam—bright lad, but always finding trouble—who got caught down there smoking with some older boys. The headteacher was absolutely livid. He called a full staff meeting on a Friday afternoon, which is just the worst possible time, isn't it? Everyone just wants to get home and start their weekend. I had plans to meet my sister for a carvery at the local pub, and I ended up being nearly an hour late. They'd run out of the roast pork by the time I got there, which is a terrible shame because their crackling is usually absolutely fantastic... \n\nAnyway, I'm sorry, I've lost the thread again. Did you ask about other rooms? No, it's really just the basement and the shed, like I mentioned. I haven't been avoiding anywhere else inside the house, if that's what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you have completely stopped doing because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Well, it's like I was saying before with the laundry and the gate. I've basically stopped doing any sort of maintenance or chores that take me near the basement or the garden shed. It's just made owning this house so much harder to manage. And far more expensive! I'm constantly having to hire people for the silliest little jobs.\n\nActually, speaking of expensive, you wouldn't believe the quote I got for getting the gutters cleaned last month. Absolute daylight robbery! The chap came round in this terribly flashy van—one of those new electric ones, you know, completely silent. You don't even hear them coming down the street. It’s quite dangerous, really. My neighbor Mrs. Higgins nearly stepped right out in front of a delivery van the other day because she didn't hear it. She’s getting on a bit, poor dear, and her hearing isn't what it used to be. I’ve told her time and time again she needs to go and get her ears syringed, but she insists on trying these home remedies she reads about in magazines. The other week she was putting warm olive oil in them, which just sounds horribly messy to me...\n\nOh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Sorry. But yes, the daily activities... I just let chores back up terribly, like the washing, and I completely avoid parts of my own property that I really ought to be using. It's just become a massive headache to keep the place running.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the spiders, such as their legs, eyes, or movement, that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I've never stopped to analyze exactly what part of them it is. Just the whole... spider-ness of them, I suppose? I just know I can't look at them. I mean, if I'm close enough to see their eyes, things have gone horribly wrong already! \n\nIt's a bit like when my sister insists on showing me photos of her new dog. She got this pug a few months ago—Archie, she calls him—and she's always zooming in on his face on her phone. \"Look at those big eyes,\" she says. But honestly, those dogs just look like they're struggling to breathe, don't they? I read an article in the Sunday paper about how they've been bred to have those flat faces, and it causes all sorts of health problems. The vet bills must be astronomical. I told her she should have got a sensible dog, like a Labrador or maybe a nice little terrier. We had a terrier when we were growing up, a Jack Russell named Barnaby. He was an absolute terror for digging up the flowerbeds, though. My dad was always furious because he spent his weekends trying to cultivate these prize-winning dahlias...\n\nSorry, what did you ask me? Right, the spiders. No, there isn't one specific thing about them. It's just the whole creature that makes my skin crawl.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any hobbies or leisure activities because of your fear of encountering spiders?\n\nPatient: Well, not really, no. I mean, most of my hobbies are indoors anyway, like reading and baking. I do love a good bake. Actually, I was just making a Victoria sponge for the school summer fete last weekend. It’s usually such a lovely event, but the weather was absolutely dreadful this year. It just poured down right as they were setting up the tombola. The poor PTA chair, Susan, was running around like a headless chicken trying to get the gazebos up. She’s a lovely woman, Susan, but terribly disorganized. She once forgot to book the bouncy castle until the day before! Can you imagine the uproar? The children would have been absolutely devastated. Luckily her husband knows a chap who runs a party hire business, so they managed to sort it out at the eleventh hour. He’s a plumber, her husband, or maybe an electrician... I remember he came round in a van once. Not one of those silent electric ones, thankfully. \n\nOh, dear, sorry. I've drifted off again, haven't I? What were we talking about? Hobbies, yes. No, I haven't stopped doing anything I do for fun because of the spiders. It's really just the basement and the shed that are the problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar reaction to any other animals that have multiple legs, like insects or centipedes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't love creepy-crawlies in general, but a centipede or a beetle doesn't send me into a panic like a spider does. I might just swat it with a magazine or something. Usually one of those home and garden magazines, actually. \n\nI've got stacks of them by the sofa. I keep meaning to go through them and cut out the recipes, but I never get around to it. There was this one issue that had a fantastic spread on different types of marmalade. Seville orange, ginger and lemon, even a grapefruit one! I tried making the grapefruit one, but it turned out terribly bitter. My husband—well, ex-husband now—he used to love bitter marmalade. He'd slather it on his toast every morning. Honestly, the amount of butter he used, it's a wonder his cholesterol wasn't through the roof. He was always going on these fad diets, though. One month it was all cabbage soup, the next he was only eating things that were green. I remember he tried to make a green smoothie once and the blender broke...\n\nSorry, what was the question? Other insects? Right. No, it's really just the spiders that cause this whole... reaction. Everything else is just a bit of a nuisance, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear or reaction to any other type of creature that isn't a spider?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, snakes are a bit unnerving, aren't they? But I wouldn't say I have an intense fear of them. I saw one on a school trip once, actually. We took Year 8 to this nature reserve in the Peak District. Beautiful place, really, all rolling hills and dry stone walls. But the bus journey was a nightmare. Little Tommy Jenkins threw up his packed lunch before we'd even reached the motorway. It was egg sandwiches, too. The smell was just awful. The driver, Colin, wasn't best pleased, I can tell you. He was a grumpy sort of chap anyway, always complaining about his sciatica. He had this special cushion he sat on, looked like a giant doughnut... \n\nAnyway, where was I? Oh, the snake. Yes, the guide pointed out an adder sunning itself on a rock. Some of the girls screamed, of course, but I just thought it looked a bit lazy. Not like a spider at all. So no, nothing else gives me that awful panicked feeling. Just the spiders.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, my friends know I don't like them, but it hasn't caused any real issues. My friend Margaret, she's actually terrified of birds. Can you believe it? Pigeons, seagulls, even little robins. We were having tea on her patio last summer—she has this gorgeous wrought-iron patio set she got at an estate sale. It took her husband three weekends to sand down and repaint it. He used this lovely duck-egg blue, though I told her a classic white might have been better for the garden. Anyway, we were having tea, and a blackbird landed on the fence, and she practically threw her Earl Grey over her shoulder trying to get inside. Ruined a perfectly good linen blouse. It was one of those M&S ones, you know, the ones that wash so well but need a good iron. I always say, if you're going to buy linen, you have to commit to the ironing board. My mother used to starch everything. Pillowcases, tablecloths, even my father's handkerchiefs... \n\nOh, I've done it again, haven't I? I'm so sorry. What was the question? Relationships? Yes, no, my fear hasn't ruined any friendships or anything like that. It's just a personal nuisance, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt afraid of other arachnids, like ticks or scorpions, in a similar way to how you feel about spiders?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say I have. Ticks are nasty little things, aren't they? Carrying diseases and such. My sister's dog, a lovely golden retriever named Barnaby, got a tick once after they went walking in the New Forest. Oh, the New Forest is stunning in the autumn. The leaves turn these brilliant shades of copper and gold. We used to rent a cottage down there when the kids were small. It had this thatched roof that looked like something out of a fairy tale, but it was terribly drafty in the winter. My daughter, Sarah, she always had to wear two pairs of socks just to walk across the kitchen floor. She was a bit sensitive to the cold, Sarah. Still is, actually. She moved to Scotland last year for university, Edinburgh, and she's constantly complaining about the wind off the North Sea. I told her to buy a proper down coat, but she insisted on this trendy little cropped jacket thing that barely covers her waist. Kids today, they'll freeze for fashion, won't they? I remember when I was her age, I had this awful bright pink parka... \n\nOh, sorry. Ticks and scorpions, you said? No, I've never seen a scorpion, thank goodness, and ticks are just gross, but they don't make my heart pound or anything. It's really just the spiders.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any activities outside of your home due to the possibility of encountering spiders?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I avoid things outside the house. I mean, they're mostly in dark corners indoors, aren't they? Or sheds. I do avoid the shed. But out and about, no. I still go to the garden centre every Sunday. It's a bit of a ritual, actually. They do a lovely carvery on a Sunday afternoon. The beef is always perfectly pink, and the Yorkshire puddings are massive—proper homemade ones, not those frozen things you get in the supermarket. I tried making my own once, but the batter just didn't rise. My mother-in-law, she had the magic touch with Yorkshires. She'd get the fat smoking hot before pouring the batter in. She used beef dripping, which I suppose isn't very healthy these days, what with cholesterol and everything. My GP was just telling me last month I need to watch my saturated fats. He suggested I switch to olive oil for everything, but you can't roast potatoes in olive oil, can you? They just don't get that lovely crispy edge. I usually buy Maris Pipers for roasting, though King Edwards are good too if you can find them...\n\nOh dear, I'm rambling about potatoes now. I do apologize. But yes, to answer your question, I don't avoid going out or doing activities because of the spiders. Just the shed, really.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the way spiders look or move that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if it's one specific thing. I mean, maybe it's all those legs? Just the way they scuttle across the floor, it's... ugh. It makes my skin crawl. It reminds me of this wind-up toy my brother had when we were kids. It was this horrid little tin crab, painted bright red. He used to wind it up and set it loose in the hallway just as I was coming out of the bathroom. He was a terror, my brother. Always playing pranks. Once, he put a plastic snake in my school bag right before a maths test. Mr. Henderson, our teacher, was not amused. He was a very strict man, Mr. Henderson. Always wore a tweed jacket with those leather elbow patches, even in the middle of July. He smelled faintly of chalk dust and peppermint. I actually saw him at the greengrocer's a few years ago. He must be in his eighties now, but he still had that same stern look about him. He was buying a massive cabbage. I remember wondering what one man could possibly do with a cabbage that size. You can only make so much coleslaw, can't you? And cabbage soup is just dreary. I tried the cabbage soup diet back in the nineties—everyone was doing it—and I swear I didn't lose a pound, just felt miserable and smelled like a boiled dinner for a week...\n\nOh, I've drifted off again, haven't I? I'm so sorry. Legs. Yes, I think it's the legs, and the way they move so fast. But I couldn't say for sure if it's just that. It's just the whole... spider-ness of them, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt afraid of other types of arachnids, like daddy long legs, in a similar way to how you feel about spiders?\n\nPatient: Daddy long legs? Oh, no, not at all. They're sort of... delicate, aren't they? Almost like they're just hovering there. They don't have that heavy, scuttling quality. We used to get them all the time in the corners of the classroom when I first started teaching. I was at this lovely little primary school in the Cotswolds back then. The building was Victorian, high ceilings, enormous windows that let in all this glorious light, but my goodness, the draughts! We had a caretaker, Mr. Higgins, who was always trying to patch up the window frames with this awful grey putty. He was a character, Mr. Higgins. He used to bring his Jack Russell, Buster, to work with him. Buster would just sleep under the radiator in the staff room all day. He was the fattest little dog I've ever seen. I think the dinner ladies were sneaking him sausages. They did a wonderful toad-in-the-hole on Thursdays, the dinner ladies. The kids absolutely loved it, though I always found the gravy a bit too salty. I have to be careful with my salt intake these days, you know. Blood pressure and all that. My husband bought me one of those fancy monitors you can use at home, but it always squeezes my arm far too tight...\n\nOh, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I do apologize. But no, daddy long legs don't bother me in the slightest. Just the spiders.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Honestly, that’s what brings me in to see you. I just keep arranging my entire life at home around not seeing them. \n\nLike the laundry, for instance. I put off doing it for days on end because the machine is down in the basement. I actually ended up buying new knickers at Marks & Spencer last Tuesday because I just couldn't face going down the stairs to wash mine! Though I have to say, M&S has really gone downhill lately, hasn't it? I remember when their cotton was so much softer. Now it's all these synthetic blends that go bobbly after just one wash. My mother used to swear by their knitwear, she had a cardigan in practically every color. She wore this lovely lemon yellow one to my graduation... \n\nOh, but anyway, where was I? Right, the house. Yes, I avoid the basement, I avoid the garden shed completely, like I mentioned. I even pay people to do small jobs that I know perfectly well how to do myself, simply because I can’t make myself go into those spaces. Like last week, the latch on the back gate was sticking. I know how to fix it, I’ve got the WD-40 sitting right on the kitchen counter, but the gate is next to all that thick ivy. So I paid a local handyman forty quid to do a five-minute job. Forty pounds! Just because I couldn't go near the fence. It's just getting ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided a specific room or area in your home for an extended period because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Well, like I just said, the basement is completely off-limits. I haven't set foot down there in absolute ages. It actually reminds me a bit of the old boiler room at the school where I teach. I teach Year 9 geography, you know, and the kids are always trying to sneak down into the boiler room during break time. Just last term, we had this one boy, Liam—bright lad, but always finding trouble—who got caught down there smoking with some older boys. The headteacher was absolutely livid. He called a full staff meeting on a Friday afternoon, which is just the worst possible time, isn't it? Everyone just wants to get home and start their weekend. I had plans to meet my sister for a carvery at the local pub, and I ended up being nearly an hour late. They'd run out of the roast pork by the time I got there, which is a terrible shame because their crackling is usually absolutely fantastic... \n\nAnyway, I'm sorry, I've lost the thread again. Did you ask about other rooms? No, it's really just the basement and the shed, like I mentioned. I haven't been avoiding anywhere else inside the house, if that's what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you have completely stopped doing because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Well, it's like I was saying before with the laundry and the gate. I've basically stopped doing any sort of maintenance or chores that take me near the basement or the garden shed. It's just made owning this house so much harder to manage. And far more expensive! I'm constantly having to hire people for the silliest little jobs.\n\nActually, speaking of expensive, you wouldn't believe the quote I got for getting the gutters cleaned last month. Absolute daylight robbery! The chap came round in this terribly flashy van—one of those new electric ones, you know, completely silent. You don't even hear them coming down the street. It’s quite dangerous, really. My neighbor Mrs. Higgins nearly stepped right out in front of a delivery van the other day because she didn't hear it. She’s getting on a bit, poor dear, and her hearing isn't what it used to be. I’ve told her time and time again she needs to go and get her ears syringed, but she insists on trying these home remedies she reads about in magazines. The other week she was putting warm olive oil in them, which just sounds horribly messy to me...\n\nOh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Sorry. But yes, the daily activities... I just let chores back up terribly, like the washing, and I completely avoid parts of my own property that I really ought to be using. It's just become a massive headache to keep the place running.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the spiders, such as their legs, eyes, or movement, that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I've never stopped to analyze exactly what part of them it is. Just the whole... spider-ness of them, I suppose? I just know I can't look at them. I mean, if I'm close enough to see their eyes, things have gone horribly wrong already! \n\nIt's a bit like when my sister insists on showing me photos of her new dog. She got this pug a few months ago—Archie, she calls him—and she's always zooming in on his face on her phone. \"Look at those big eyes,\" she says. But honestly, those dogs just look like they're struggling to breathe, don't they? I read an article in the Sunday paper about how they've been bred to have those flat faces, and it causes all sorts of health problems. The vet bills must be astronomical. I told her she should have got a sensible dog, like a Labrador or maybe a nice little terrier. We had a terrier when we were growing up, a Jack Russell named Barnaby. He was an absolute terror for digging up the flowerbeds, though. My dad was always furious because he spent his weekends trying to cultivate these prize-winning dahlias...\n\nSorry, what did you ask me? Right, the spiders. No, there isn't one specific thing about them. It's just the whole creature that makes my skin crawl.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any hobbies or leisure activities because of your fear of encountering spiders?\n\nPatient: Well, not really, no. I mean, most of my hobbies are indoors anyway, like reading and baking. I do love a good bake. Actually, I was just making a Victoria sponge for the school summer fete last weekend. It’s usually such a lovely event, but the weather was absolutely dreadful this year. It just poured down right as they were setting up the tombola. The poor PTA chair, Susan, was running around like a headless chicken trying to get the gazebos up. She’s a lovely woman, Susan, but terribly disorganized. She once forgot to book the bouncy castle until the day before! Can you imagine the uproar? The children would have been absolutely devastated. Luckily her husband knows a chap who runs a party hire business, so they managed to sort it out at the eleventh hour. He’s a plumber, her husband, or maybe an electrician... I remember he came round in a van once. Not one of those silent electric ones, thankfully. \n\nOh, dear, sorry. I've drifted off again, haven't I? What were we talking about? Hobbies, yes. No, I haven't stopped doing anything I do for fun because of the spiders. It's really just the basement and the shed that are the problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar reaction to any other animals that have multiple legs, like insects or centipedes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't love creepy-crawlies in general, but a centipede or a beetle doesn't send me into a panic like a spider does. I might just swat it with a magazine or something. Usually one of those home and garden magazines, actually. \n\nI've got stacks of them by the sofa. I keep meaning to go through them and cut out the recipes, but I never get around to it. There was this one issue that had a fantastic spread on different types of marmalade. Seville orange, ginger and lemon, even a grapefruit one! I tried making the grapefruit one, but it turned out terribly bitter. My husband—well, ex-husband now—he used to love bitter marmalade. He'd slather it on his toast every morning. Honestly, the amount of butter he used, it's a wonder his cholesterol wasn't through the roof. He was always going on these fad diets, though. One month it was all cabbage soup, the next he was only eating things that were green. I remember he tried to make a green smoothie once and the blender broke...\n\nSorry, what was the question? Other insects? Right. No, it's really just the spiders that cause this whole... reaction. Everything else is just a bit of a nuisance, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear or reaction to any other type of creature that isn't a spider?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, snakes are a bit unnerving, aren't they? But I wouldn't say I have an intense fear of them. I saw one on a school trip once, actually. We took Year 8 to this nature reserve in the Peak District. Beautiful place, really, all rolling hills and dry stone walls. But the bus journey was a nightmare. Little Tommy Jenkins threw up his packed lunch before we'd even reached the motorway. It was egg sandwiches, too. The smell was just awful. The driver, Colin, wasn't best pleased, I can tell you. He was a grumpy sort of chap anyway, always complaining about his sciatica. He had this special cushion he sat on, looked like a giant doughnut... \n\nAnyway, where was I? Oh, the snake. Yes, the guide pointed out an adder sunning itself on a rock. Some of the girls screamed, of course, but I just thought it looked a bit lazy. Not like a spider at all. So no, nothing else gives me that awful panicked feeling. Just the spiders.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, my friends know I don't like them, but it hasn't caused any real issues. My friend Margaret, she's actually terrified of birds. Can you believe it? Pigeons, seagulls, even little robins. We were having tea on her patio last summer—she has this gorgeous wrought-iron patio set she got at an estate sale. It took her husband three weekends to sand down and repaint it. He used this lovely duck-egg blue, though I told her a classic white might have been better for the garden. Anyway, we were having tea, and a blackbird landed on the fence, and she practically threw her Earl Grey over her shoulder trying to get inside. Ruined a perfectly good linen blouse. It was one of those M&S ones, you know, the ones that wash so well but need a good iron. I always say, if you're going to buy linen, you have to commit to the ironing board. My mother used to starch everything. Pillowcases, tablecloths, even my father's handkerchiefs... \n\nOh, I've done it again, haven't I? I'm so sorry. What was the question? Relationships? Yes, no, my fear hasn't ruined any friendships or anything like that. It's just a personal nuisance, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt afraid of other arachnids, like ticks or scorpions, in a similar way to how you feel about spiders?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say I have. Ticks are nasty little things, aren't they? Carrying diseases and such. My sister's dog, a lovely golden retriever named Barnaby, got a tick once after they went walking in the New Forest. Oh, the New Forest is stunning in the autumn. The leaves turn these brilliant shades of copper and gold. We used to rent a cottage down there when the kids were small. It had this thatched roof that looked like something out of a fairy tale, but it was terribly drafty in the winter. My daughter, Sarah, she always had to wear two pairs of socks just to walk across the kitchen floor. She was a bit sensitive to the cold, Sarah. Still is, actually. She moved to Scotland last year for university, Edinburgh, and she's constantly complaining about the wind off the North Sea. I told her to buy a proper down coat, but she insisted on this trendy little cropped jacket thing that barely covers her waist. Kids today, they'll freeze for fashion, won't they? I remember when I was her age, I had this awful bright pink parka... \n\nOh, sorry. Ticks and scorpions, you said? No, I've never seen a scorpion, thank goodness, and ticks are just gross, but they don't make my heart pound or anything. It's really just the spiders.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any activities outside of your home due to the possibility of encountering spiders?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I avoid things outside the house. I mean, they're mostly in dark corners indoors, aren't they? Or sheds. I do avoid the shed. But out and about, no. I still go to the garden centre every Sunday. It's a bit of a ritual, actually. They do a lovely carvery on a Sunday afternoon. The beef is always perfectly pink, and the Yorkshire puddings are massive—proper homemade ones, not those frozen things you get in the supermarket. I tried making my own once, but the batter just didn't rise. My mother-in-law, she had the magic touch with Yorkshires. She'd get the fat smoking hot before pouring the batter in. She used beef dripping, which I suppose isn't very healthy these days, what with cholesterol and everything. My GP was just telling me last month I need to watch my saturated fats. He suggested I switch to olive oil for everything, but you can't roast potatoes in olive oil, can you? They just don't get that lovely crispy edge. I usually buy Maris Pipers for roasting, though King Edwards are good too if you can find them...\n\nOh dear, I'm rambling about potatoes now. I do apologize. But yes, to answer your question, I don't avoid going out or doing activities because of the spiders. Just the shed, really.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the way spiders look or move that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure if it's one specific thing. I mean, maybe it's all those legs? Just the way they scuttle across the floor, it's... ugh. It makes my skin crawl. It reminds me of this wind-up toy my brother had when we were kids. It was this horrid little tin crab, painted bright red. He used to wind it up and set it loose in the hallway just as I was coming out of the bathroom. He was a terror, my brother. Always playing pranks. Once, he put a plastic snake in my school bag right before a maths test. Mr. Henderson, our teacher, was not amused. He was a very strict man, Mr. Henderson. Always wore a tweed jacket with those leather elbow patches, even in the middle of July. He smelled faintly of chalk dust and peppermint. I actually saw him at the greengrocer's a few years ago. He must be in his eighties now, but he still had that same stern look about him. He was buying a massive cabbage. I remember wondering what one man could possibly do with a cabbage that size. You can only make so much coleslaw, can't you? And cabbage soup is just dreary. I tried the cabbage soup diet back in the nineties—everyone was doing it—and I swear I didn't lose a pound, just felt miserable and smelled like a boiled dinner for a week...\n\nOh, I've drifted off again, haven't I? I'm so sorry. Legs. Yes, I think it's the legs, and the way they move so fast. But I couldn't say for sure if it's just that. It's just the whole... spider-ness of them, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt afraid of other types of arachnids, like daddy long legs, in a similar way to how you feel about spiders?\n\nPatient: Daddy long legs? Oh, no, not at all. They're sort of... delicate, aren't they? Almost like they're just hovering there. They don't have that heavy, scuttling quality. We used to get them all the time in the corners of the classroom when I first started teaching. I was at this lovely little primary school in the Cotswolds back then. The building was Victorian, high ceilings, enormous windows that let in all this glorious light, but my goodness, the draughts! We had a caretaker, Mr. Higgins, who was always trying to patch up the window frames with this awful grey putty. He was a character, Mr. Higgins. He used to bring his Jack Russell, Buster, to work with him. Buster would just sleep under the radiator in the staff room all day. He was the fattest little dog I've ever seen. I think the dinner ladies were sneaking him sausages. They did a wonderful toad-in-the-hole on Thursdays, the dinner ladies. The kids absolutely loved it, though I always found the gravy a bit too salty. I have to be careful with my salt intake these days, you know. Blood pressure and all that. My husband bought me one of those fancy monitors you can use at home, but it always squeezes my arm far too tight...\n\nOh, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I do apologize. But no, daddy long legs don't bother me in the slightest. Just the spiders.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I can't say that I have. I mean, if it's not a spider, it's just not a spider, is it? Though, funnily enough, I did once mistake a bit of fluff for one. It was just sitting there on the carpet, right near the skirting board in the hallway. We have this lovely Axminster carpet in the hall—it's a sort of deep burgundy with a gold floral pattern. We got it on sale at John Lewis years ago. The salesman, oh, what was his name... Derek, I think? He was ever so helpful. He told us that with a patterned carpet, you don't notice the dirt half as much, which is brilliant when you've got people tramping in and out all day. Although, my husband, bless him, he never remembers to wipe his feet. He’s got these heavy gardening boots that he leaves right by the front door, and the mud just dries and flakes off everywhere. I bought him one of those boot scrapers shaped like a hedgehog—cast iron, very heavy—but he says it's too fiddly to use. I suppose I can't complain too much, he does keep the roses looking spectacular. We've got a climbing rose, a 'New Dawn', I think it's called, that just covers the trellis by the kitchen window in the summer. The scent is absolutely heavenly, especially after a bit of rain... 

Goodness, I've wandered right off the path again, haven't I? I am sorry. But no, to answer your question, nothing else really sets me off. Just actual spiders.
